Let me think about the proof strategy for STLC CBV strong normalization with locally nameless.

The language: locally nameless with free variables (atoms) and bound variables (de Bruijn indices). Types Bool and arrow. Step is CBV: beta on values, plus congruence on application (App1 and App2). Values are lambdas (locally closed), true, false.

We need: every closed well-typed term is strongly normalizing.

Standard approach: Logical relation / reducibility candidates for CBV STLC. In CBV, the standard Girard-Tait style logical relation works if defined as: 
- SN at Bool = SN.
- SN at arrow A -> B = terms t that are SN and for all u, SN_A u -> SN_B (t u).

Actually for CBV, the standard candidate definition: a term is in the reducibility candidate for A->B if it's SN and whenever it steps to a term t' (strongly normalizing... hmm) — let's recall the standard "hereditarily strongly normalizing" definition for CBV:

Define:
- SN(t) := strongly_normalizing t.
- R_Bool = SN.
- R_{A->B}(t) := SN(t) ∧ ∀ u, R_A(u) → R_B(t u).

But for the fundamental theorem, in the lambda case, we need: lambda x:T1, t ∈ R_{T1->T2} given that for all closed u ∈ R_T1, open t u ∈ R_T2. We need SN(λ.t). A lambda is SN if all its reducts are SN. Reducts of the lambda itself: none unless... hmm wait, is the lambda body reduced? No! In CBV small-step semantics, step doesn't reduce inside a lambda. `tm_abs` is a value and has no step rule. So SN(λ.t) trivially holds because there are no steps from it. Wait, but strongly_normalizing is defined inductively as all one-step reducts SN. Since λ is a value with no steps, SN immediately. Great. But careful: a lambda itself could be applied, but SN of lambda = no steps from it.

Hmm, but that's the beauty of CBV: values are strongly normalizing (no reductions inside lambdas).

Actually, then the logical relation for arrow needs the closure under applications: t ∈ R_{A->B} iff SN(t) and for all u ∈ R_A, t u ∈ R_B. But if t is a closed value lambda, t u reduces to open t u which must be in R_B. That is from the abstraction typing.

Wait but there's a subtlety: t ∈ R_{A->B} for arbitrary terms, not just values; the fundamental theorem gives for t of arrow type that t ∈ R_{A->B}. For the application case (T_App with t1: A->B, t2: A), the fundamental theorem gives t1 ∈ R_{A->B} and t2 ∈ R_A, hence t1 t2 ∈ R_B ⊆ SN. Good.

For the lambda case: need λ:T1.t ∈ R_{T1->T2}: SN(λ.t) (trivial) and for all u ∈ R_T1, (λ.t) u ∈ R_{T2}. Hmm, (λ.t) u steps to open t u, but also might have other reducts? Step rule for app (λ.t) u with value u: ST_AppAbs gives open t u. But also ST_App2 with value v1 = λ.t: if u -> u', then (λ.t) u -> (λ.t) u'. Also ST_App1: λ.t -> t1' impossible since λ.t has no steps (no rule for lambda reduction). Wait — actually ST_App1 requires t1 --> t1', and λ.t is a value and has no step. So only ST_AppAbs (needs value u) and ST_App2 (needs u --> u').

To show (λ.t) u ∈ R_{T2} where R_{T2} is a candidate (SN + maybe a closure condition), we need SN((λ.t)u) and the arrow case? Actually R_{T2} for T2 = Bool is SN; for T2 = arrow it's SN plus application closure. Hmm, showing the application closure for (λ.t)u requires: for all w ∈ R_B, ((λ.t) u) w ∈ R_C. That's harder from just open t u ∈ R_{T2}? Hmm.

Wait, the standard approach uses a different relation for CBV: hereditary strong normalization "HSN" or the notion of "strongly normalizing terms closed under expansion". A robust approach for CBV: define reducibility candidates as sets of strongly normalizing terms closed under "forward closure" (if t ∈ R and t --> t' then t' ∈ R) and maybe under "expansion" for neutral terms. But the classic issue: for CBV, the lambda case of the fundamental lemma requires that λ.t is in R_{A->B}, and R_{A->B} includes application closure for all arguments in R_A. But note that (λ.t) u for u ∈ R_A: we need it in R_B. We know u is SN, so (λ.t)u is SN assuming open t u is SN... hmm but open t u ∈ R_B needs u to be a *closed* term? Or maybe not closed—the logical relation is defined on open terms with a closing substitution.

Actually the standard approach with locally nameless: define the logical relation on open terms via closing substitution? Another approach: define the relation on terms that may have free variables, via a valuation/context interpretation: for a context Gamma, an environment sigma mapping each fvar to a term, and the notion "sigma satisfies Gamma" and "t is in R_T under sigma". Then the fundamental theorem is for open terms.

Let me think carefully. Since locally nameless STLC with free atoms and de Bruijn bound vars, and terms in judgements are locally closed, the T_Abs rule uses open with fvar x. We can handle this in two common ways:

Approach A: Define semantics on closed terms only, and for the lambda case use the T_Abs premise instantiated at a fresh fvar x; since the term open t (fvar x) is not closed (it has fvar), we need a semantic relation on open terms with free variables. Then fundamental lemma is stated with a context and closing substitution.

Approach B: Avoid free vars in the semantic relation by using de Bruijn... but the supplied typing rules use fvar opening, so we need to deal with free variables.

Alternative standard approach: Define the logical relation semantically as least fixed point via inductive definition:
- R_Bool = SN.
- R_{A->B}(t) := SN(t) ∧ (∀ t', t --> t' -> R_{A->B}(t')) ∧ ∀ u, R_A(u) -> SN(t u) ... hmm.

Actually, for CBV, a very clean approach is to use the notion of "strongly normalizing" plus hereditary closure under application at each type, defined as an inductive family indexed by type, for arbitrary terms:

Inductive R : ty -> tm -> Prop :=
| R_Bool_intro : forall t, SN t -> R Bool t
| R_Arrow_intro : forall A B t,
    SN t ->
    (forall t', t --> t' -> R (A->B) t') ->   (* forward closure *)
    (forall u, R A u -> R B (t u)) ->          (* application closure *)
    R (A->B) t.

Hmm, but the fundamental theorem at T_Abs requires showing R (A->B) (λ.t). We have SN (λ.t) trivially. Forward closure trivial (no steps). Application closure: for u ∈ R A, need R B ((λ.t) u). Now, (λ.t) u: if u is a value, it steps to open t u; if u steps to u', it steps to (λ.t) u'. We know... hmm, we'd need R B ((λ.t)u). Since R B is closed under forward steps if R B has that clause? For B = Bool, R Bool = SN, and SN((λ.t)u) follows from SN(u) and SN(open t u) if u is a value; but if u is not a value, u steps... This requires a general lemma: if u is SN and for all values v with u -->* v, open t v is SN (or in R B), then (λ.t)u is SN. This is the standard "SN of application" lemma requiring the substitution/opening property. Hmm.

But note: in T_Abs, we only get to know that for all x not in L, open t (fvar x) is well-typed. With locally nameless and fresh fvar, we don't have "u ranging over arbitrary terms"; we have the abstraction property at free variables. So to prove the application closure of λ.t at arbitrary u ∈ R_A, we need to relate open t u (u arbitrary) to open t (fvar x) under a substitution mapping x to u. That's the classic substitution lemma for the logical relation on open terms. Hmm, but we can perhaps avoid by defining the relation on open terms with a closing environment.

The cleanest way: Logical relation for open terms via closing substitution (semantic typing), where terms may contain free atoms:

Define `sem_ty (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop`:
- Bool: SN t
- Arrow A B: SN t ∧ ∀ u, sem_ty A u -> sem_ty B (tm_app t u)

and the context interpretation: for a context Gamma and substitution sigma : atom -> tm (mapping free vars to terms), define `sem_ctx Gamma sigma := forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> sem_ty T (sigma x)`.

Then fundamental theorem: if Gamma |-- t \in T, then for all sigma with sem_ctx Gamma sigma, sem_ty T (subst sigma t) — where subst replaces fvar x with sigma x (bound vars unaffected). But wait, terms are locally closed, so no free bound variables issues. Hmm, but the target theorem is about `empty |-- t \in T`, with empty context; we could take sigma to be the identity substitution (fvar x ↦ fvar x) or any substitution. Then sem_ty T t => SN t.

If subst sigma (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma t1) (fvar x) and subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma t1) u, then we get the desired term open (subst sigma t1) u = subst sigma' (open t1 (fvar x)) (this identity is true since x fresh / not in L and substitution compositionality). Hmm, so the relation isn't obviously closed under expansion. Let's think about the right definition for CBV normalization.

Standard technique: "strong normalization" in CBV often uses the notion of "hereditarily normalizing" with an explicit inductive definition that includes closure under expansion for "neutral" terms, or uses Girard's reducibility candidates adapted with a "head expansion" property.

Inductive R : ty -> tm -> Prop :=

Lemma beta_expand : forall A B body u,
  lc_at 1 body ->
  (forall v, C A v -> C B (open body v)) ->
  C A u ->
  C B (app (abs A body) u).

  - LC: from lc_at 1 body (abs LC) and C_A u gives LC u.

So reducts app(abs)u' with u --> u'. We need C_B(app(abs)u'). Apply beta_expand recursively... on what? u' is a reduct of SN u? We know C_A u gives SN u, so SN u'. But beta_expand's argument needs a well-founded induction on SN u to handle this recursive case. Hmm, but actually the recursive calls on u' are along u --> u'; since SN u, well-founded. But we also need C_A u' to apply the lemma. Forward closure of C_A gives C_A u'. So prove beta_expand by induction on SN u (using SN's induction principle), with H global and lc body. Then in the u-not-value case, use IH at u' (SN u') with forward-closed C_A u'. ✓.
     
     Note in the u-value case, no recursive call. So induction on SN u works.

  - ~value(app(abs)u): obvious (no value constructor for app).
  - Then neutral_expand gives C_B(app(abs)u). ✓

Also note: the IH gives C_T2 (subst θ' (open t1 (fvar x))), but we need C_T2 (open (subst θ t1) v). We need the syntactic identity. Let's prove it:

Lemma subst_open_fvar : forall t θ v x,
  x ∉ fv t ->
  subst (update_env θ x v) (open t (fvar x)) = open (subst θ t) v.

Hmm, wait: θ' = x ↦ v as a function atom -> tm: fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then v else θ y. And `open t (fvar x)` replaces bvar 0 with fvar x. Let's verify the identity by induction on t:
 - t = bvar i: open t (fvar x) = if i =? 0 then fvar x else bvar i. Case i=0: LHS = subst θ' (fvar x) = v. RHS: subst θ (bvar 0) = bvar 0, then open (bvar 0) v = v. ✓ (Note: if i=0, then x ∉ fv t is vacuous since fv(bvar 0) = [].)
   Case i≠0: LHS = subst θ' (bvar i) = bvar i. RHS: open (bvar i) v = bvar i (since i≠0). ✓.
 - t = fvar y: open t (fvar x) = fvar y. LHS = subst θ' (fvar y) = if y =? x then v else θ y. Since x ∉ fv t = {y}, y ≠ x, so LHS = θ y. RHS = subst θ (fvar y) = θ y, then open (θ y) v = θ y — wait, open (fvar y) v = fvar y; open applies to the term θ y which is an arbitrary term; opening at depth 0 of θ y replaces free bvar 0 occurrences! Uh oh: RHS = open (subst θ t) v = open_rec 0 v (θ y), which may differ from θ y if θ y contains free bvar 0. Hmm!

That's a problem. Let's recompute: open (subst θ t) v = open_rec 0 v (θ y). If θ y contains bvar 0 free, opening replaces it. But LHS = θ y (since y≠x). So the identity fails unless θ y is locally closed at 0? Hmm. Wait, but in our intended use, θ maps atoms to closed terms? The fundamental theorem's θ satisfies C (Γ y) (θ y), and C gives LC, so θ y is locally closed at 0. Then open (θ y) v = θ y, and the identity holds. So we need an additional hypothesis: all θ y are locally closed (or at least LC at 0). Actually, wait: is it true that open_rec 0 v t = t when LC t? lc_at 0 t means all free bvars i have i < 0 — none. open_rec 0 only replaces bvar 0. So yes, open 0 v t = t for lc_at 0 t. Need lemma.

But more generally, substitution under binders: subst θ (open t (fvar x)) — in the abs case, open goes deeper with k=1, while subst doesn't care. Let's do the induction carefully with a generalized statement, maybe:

Lemma subst_open_gen : forall t θ v x,
  x ∉ fv t ->
  (forall y, lc_at 0 (θ y)) ->
  subst θ' (open t (fvar x)) = open (subst θ t) v
where θ' = x↦v and v lc_at 0? Hmm, the RHS opening at depth 0 of subst θ t: inside subst θ t, bound variables from t remain; and θ images are LC so no accidental bvar 0 free. But under binders in t, open_rec goes to depth k; subst θ t keeps binders; opening (subst θ t) v at 0 only affects bvar 0 at depth 0. Substitution images are inserted at any depth (e.g., under an abs, an image θ y sits at depth 0 relative to the binder? Actually subst doesn't shift; subst θ (tm_abs T body) = tm_abs T (subst θ body). The image θ y in body is under a binder, but de Bruijn indices in θ y are not shifted. So if θ y contains bvar 0 free, it becomes bound-looking under the binder. This is the usual issue that substitution of open terms needs care; but if θ y is LC (no free bvars), no issue.

So requiring θ y LC suffices for the identity to hold in all contexts? Let's redo the induction: In the abs case, t = tm_abs T t'. open t (fvar x) = tm_abs T (open_rec 1 (fvar x) t'). subst θ' of that = tm_abs T (subst θ' (open_rec 1 (fvar x) t')). RHS = open (subst θ (tm_abs T t')) v = open (tm_abs T (subst θ t')) v = tm_abs T (open_rec 1 v (subst θ t')). So need: subst θ' (open_rec 1 (fvar x) t') = open_rec 1 v (subst θ t'). This is a generalized statement with opening at depth k: subst θ' (open_rec k (fvar x) t') = open_rec k v (subst θ t')? Hmm, but open_rec k (fvar x) replaces bvar k; subst doesn't affect bvars. And open_rec k v (subst θ t') replaces bvar k in subst θ t'. Since θ images are LC (no free bvars at all), they contain no bvar k, so replacement doesn't touch them. And the bvar k occurrences in t' are replaced by fvar x, then substituted to v; RHS: bvar k replaced by v. So this works with LC images. The freshness x ∉ fv t' is needed for the fvar case; under binders, fv unchanged.

So the generalized lemma:
Lemma subst_open_rec : forall k t θ v x,
  x ∉ fv t ->
  (forall y, lc_at 0 (θ y)) ->
  lc_at 0 v ->  (* v ∈ C_T1 implies LC *)
  subst θ' (open_rec k (fvar x) t) = open_rec k v (subst θ t)
where θ' = x ↦ v.

Hmm, but wait: open_rec k (fvar x) t replaces free bvar k. In the bvar case i: if i = k then fvar x else bvar i. LHS: if i=k then subst θ' (fvar x) = v (since θ' x = v; note x ∉ fv (bvar i) vacuous). RHS: open_rec k v (subst θ (bvar i)) = open_rec k v (bvar i) = if i=k then v else bvar i. ✓.
 fvar y: LHS = subst θ' (fvar y) = if y=x then v else θ y; x ∉ fv t so y≠x → θ y. RHS = open_rec k v (θ y), which equals θ y if θ y has no free bvar k — since θ y LC at 0, it has no free bvars at all. ✓.
 app: straightforward.
 abs: both sides push k to k+1 and subst inside. Need IH with k+1 and t' ; x ∉ fv t' ✓. ✓.
 true/false: ✓.

Great. So the identity holds with the hypothesis that all θ images are lc_at 0.

Now, the fundamental theorem will need θ images to be LC, which follows from C (Γ y) (θ y): we define C to include LC. Good.

Now, let's formulate everything for our actual language and prove it in Rocq. Details:

Definitions:

1. `fv (t : tm) : list atom` computing free variables (fvars), and a lemma `fv_open` or `fresh` existence. Alternatively avoid needing a concrete fv function by using `L` from T_Abs plus a list of all atoms in t1? We need freshness w.r.t. fv t1; fv t1 is finite; we can compute it. Let's define:

Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true | tm_false => nil
  end.

Freshness lemma: exists x, ~ In x (L ++ fv t). Easy by induction on L? We can use a function `max_atoms` or use `more_than_all`. Simpler: prove `exists x, ~ In x L` for any list (e.g., max L + 1) and similarly for L ++ fv t. Standard: define `fresh (L : list atom) : atom := S (fold_right max 0 L)`? Then prove not in L using Nat.ltb? Let's do:

Fixpoint max_list (L : list nat) : nat :=
  match L with [] => 0 | x::xs => max x (max_list xs) end.
Lemma max_list_ge : forall L x, In x L -> x <= max_list L.
Then fresh = S (max_list L); S (max_list L) ∉ L. Good. Or we can use `List.fold_right max 0 L`. Let's just define our own.

Alternatively, use the classical `AtomSet`? Keep it simple with lists.

2. Substitution:
Definition subst (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.
Note: this is not capture-avoiding because de Bruijn indices in images are not shifted. But since images are LC (no free bvars), it's fine. Define it as a Fixpoint.

3. LC lemmas:
- `lc_at_mono : forall k k' t, k <= k' -> lc_at k t -> lc_at k' t`? Actually lc_at k gives free bvars < k. Monotone in k.
- `lc_at_0_open_rec`: if lc_at 0 t then open_rec k v t = t for any k? lc_at 0 means all free bvars < 0, none; open_rec k only replaces bvar k; so yes, open_rec k v t = t. Wait, under binders, lc_at 0 t means at depth, bvars < depth? Let's check lc_at definition: lc_at k (tm_abs T t1) requires lc_at (S k) t1; lc_bvar k i requires i < k. So lc_at 0 (tm_abs T t1) needs lc_at 1 t1: bvar 0 free in t1 is allowed (i < 1). Let's verify: open_rec 0 v t only replaces *free* bvar 0 (at top level), and lc_at 0 t means no free bvars at all, so open_rec 0 v t = t. Yes! Because open_rec k under a binder uses S k, so it only replaces free occurrences of bvar k. lc_at 0 t means every free bvar i satisfies i < 0 — impossible; so no free bvars, so open_rec 0 v t = t. Good. More generally lc_at k t -> open_rec k v t = t (since free bvars < k, none equals k). Let's prove: `lc_at_open_rec_id : forall k v t, lc_at k t -> open_rec k v t = t`. Also we need: `lc_at` of open_rec preserves? For substitution: if t is lc_at k and all sigma images lc_at 0, then subst sigma t is lc_at k? 4. Now the main C relation. Let's define it as a Fixpoint? It's recursive on the type, and in the arrow case calls C on subcomponents and on arbitrary terms. Define:

Fixpoint C (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => lc_at 0 t /\ SN t
  | Ty_Arrow A B => lc_at 0 t /\ SN t /\ (forall u, C A u -> C B (tm_app t u))
  end.

Coq accepts this since recursive calls are on subterms A and B; the term arguments vary but the type decreases. Good. Note: `SN t` here is `strongly_normalizing t`.

Then prove:
(a) C_lc : forall T t, C T t -> lc_at 0 t. (by destruct T; simpl; tauto)
(b) C_sn : forall T t, C T t -> SN t.
(c) C_step : forall T t t', C T t -> step t t' -> C T t'. (forward closure) — by induction on T.
    Bool: lc_at 0 t' from step_preserves_lc; SN from SN's definition: SN t = SN_intro t (forall t', step t t' -> SN t'); destruct SN t to get. Hmm, `strongly_normalizing` is an Inductive with one constructor; destruct (Hsn : SN t) gives the premise. But be careful: SN is an inductive proposition; is it well-founded enough to destruct? Yes, destruct gives the field.
    Arrow A B: need lc_at 0 t' ✓; SN t' ✓; forall u, C A u -> C B (app t' u). Given C A u, we have C B (app t u) from H's third component. And step (app t u) (app t' u) via ST_App1 with lc_at 0 u (from C_lc A u) and Hstep: step t t'. Then apply IH_B: C B (app t u) -> step (app t u) (app t' u) -> C B (app t' u). Wait, the induction on T gives IH for B: "forall t t', C B t -> step t t' -> C B t'". Yes, structurally B is a subterm of Arrow A B. ✓. Note we don't need IH_A.
(d) C_neutral : forall T t, lc_at 0 t -> (forall t', step t t' -> C T t') -> ~ value t -> C T t.
    Prove by induction on T.
    - Bool: split; lc ✓; SN by SN_intro: for all t', step t t' -> SN t' from H t' Hstep and C_sn (IH? no, C_sn lemma). ✓.
    - Arrow A B: need lc; SN; and forall u, C A u -> C B (app t u).
      * SN: for all t', step t t' -> SN t'; from H t' Hstep and C_sn (C_step? no) C_sn B (H t' Hstep). ✓.
      * Application closure: given u with C A u. Need C B (app t u). Apply IH_B (structural: B is a subterm of A->B) to the term app t u:
          - lc_at 0 (app t u): from lc t and C_lc A u.
          - all step reducts of app t u are in C B: take t2' with step (app t u) t2'. Need C B t2'. Inversion on step:
            + ST_AppAbs T' body v with t = tm_abs T' body, u = v, value v: contradicts ~value t (t is abs → value, provided locally_closed (tm_abs T' body); we have lc_at 0 t = lc_at 1 body → v_abs applies). Contradiction.
            + ST_App1 t1 t1' t2 with t = t1, t2 = u, step t t1', lc_at 0 u: t2' = app t1' u. C B (app t1' u) by H t1' Hstep : C (A->B) (t1') and application closure with C A u. ✓
            + ST_App2 v1 t2 t2' with t = v1 value — contradicts ~value t. Contradiction.
          - ~value (app t u): app is never a value. ✓
          Then IH_B gives C B (app t u). ✓ 
      Note: IH_B is the induction hypothesis for type B in the type induction of C_neutral. Good.
      
      Wait, in the ST_App1 case, H t1' Hstep gives C (A->B) t1', and its application closure gives C B (app t1' u). ✓.
      
So C_neutral proven by induction on T. Good.

(f) Fundamental theorem:

Statement: forall Γ t T, has_type Γ t T -> forall θ, (forall x T', Γ x = Some T' -> C T' (θ x)) -> (forall x, lc_at 0 (θ x)) -> C T (subst θ t).

Actually the LC condition on θ follows from the first: C T' (θ x) -> lc_at 0 (θ x) by C_lc. But when Γ x = None, we don't get LC for θ x. Do we need all θ x LC? The identity subst_open_rec needs images LC only for fvars occurring in t, which are in Γ (by typing). But our statement quantifies θ over all atoms; to have the identity for t1 in the abs case, we need θ' images LC; θ' x = v LC, θ y for y in fv t1 — we need y ∈ dom Γ? Hmm, t1 is the body; its free variables may include x itself? x is chosen fresh w.r.t. fv t1, so not. Other fvars of t1: are they in Γ? The premise types open t1 (fvar x) under x|->T1; Γ, so every fvar occurring in it is in the domain of x|->T1;Γ. Since x ∉ fv t1, fvars of t1 are in dom Γ. So θ y is LC via C. But to apply the syntactic identity lemma, we need "forall y, lc_at 0 (θ' y)" globally, not just on fv t1. θ' = update θ x v; for y not in dom Γ and not x, θ' y = θ y might not be LC. Does the identity lemma subst_open_rec need all images LC, or only those occurring? Our proof above of subst_open_rec using "forall y, lc_at 0 (θ y)" — in the fvar case it needs θ y LC for the specific y = the fvar; other cases don't. We could weaken the hypothesis to: forall y, In y (fv t) -> lc_at 0 (θ y). Let's check the proof: only the fvar case uses it, for y ∈ fv (fvar y). So yes, we can state:

Lemma subst_open_rec : forall k t θ v x,
  x ∉ fv t ->
  (forall y, In y (fv t) -> lc_at 0 (θ y)) ->
  lc_at 0 v ->
  subst (update_env θ x v) (open_rec k (fvar x) t) = open_rec k v (subst θ t).

Wait, but in the abs case, we use IH on the body t' with the same θ and x; fv t' = fv t, so the hypothesis transfers. Good. Actually careful: the abs case's LHS is subst θ' (open_rec k (fvar x) (tm_abs T t')) = subst θ' (tm_abs T (open_rec (S k) (fvar x) t')) = tm_abs T (subst θ' (open_rec (S k) (fvar x) t')). RHS = open_rec k v (subst θ (tm_abs T t')) = open_rec k v (tm_abs T (subst θ t')) = tm_abs T (open_rec (S k) v (subst θ t')). So apply IH with k+1; hypotheses: x ∉ fv t' (same as fv t) ✓, forall y ∈ fv t', lc θ y ✓. Good.

Hmm, but for the fvar y case, we need y ≠ x from x ∉ fv t. ✓.

So in the fundamental theorem, we can require "forall y, In y (fv t) -> lc_at 0 (θ y)"? But fv t varies with t; the abs case needs it for the body t1, and the fvars of t1 are in dom Γ. Hmm, but the induction hypothesis is applied to the open term open t1 (fvar x) with substitution θ' = update θ x v. We need to provide for all y ∈ fv (open t1 (fvar x)), lc_at 0 (θ' y). fv (open t1 (fvar x)) ⊆ fv t1 ∪ {x} (plus maybe?). If y ∈ fv t1: y ∈ dom Γ (since typing of open t1 (fvar x) under x|->T1;Γ and x ∉ fv t1 ensures fvar y in the term is typed via Γ y = Some...), so C gives LC θ y = θ' y (y≠x). If y = x: θ' x = v LC. Induction principle for `has_type`: it's an inductive Prop with a premise that is a function (forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type ...). Coq generates the induction principle with IH for that? Actually, for the T_Abs constructor, the argument is `(forall x : atom, ~ In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (tm_fvar x)) T2)`, and the induction principle gives, in the T_Abs case, a hypothesis `(forall x H, IHT2 ... )`? Hmm. Coq's automatic scheme generation handles strictly positive occurrences in product types: it produces for each recursive occurrence under binders an IH.   T_Abs case: forall L Gamma T1 T2 t1, (forall x (Hx : ~ In x L), has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (tm_fvar x)) T2) -> (forall x (Hx : ~ In x L), P (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (tm_fvar x)) T2) -> P Gamma (tm_abs T1 t1) (Ty_Arrow T1 T2).
Actually, I think Coq generates the IH as `forall x Hx, P (update ...) (open ...) T2`. Let me recall precisely. For inductives with premises that are themselves functions whose codomain is the inductive, Coq's scheme does add induction hypotheses. Example:
Inductive foo : nat -> Prop :=
| foo_intro : forall n, (forall m, foo m) -> foo n.
The generated foo_ind: forall P, (forall n, (forall m, foo m) -> (forall m, P m) -> P n) -> forall n, foo n -> P n. Yes, I'm fairly confident.
But here the occurrence is under `forall x, ~ In x L ->`, so likely: (forall x (H : ~ In x L), P (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2). Good.

Another subtlety: In the fundamental theorem, the statement quantifies θ, and the induction on Hty must generalize θ. The standard approach:
Lemma fundamental : forall Gamma t T, has_type Gamma t T -> forall theta, lc_all theta -> satisfies Gamma theta -> C T (subst theta t).
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T Hty.
  induction Hty; intros theta Hlc Hsat.
  ...
In the T_Abs case, IH is (forall x Hx, forall theta, lc_all theta -> satisfies (x|->T1;Gamma) theta -> C T2 (subst theta (open t1 (fvar x)))) — need to check the exact form. Then we apply IH x Hx theta' Hlc' Hsat'.

Now, the statement `satisfies`: `Definition sat (Gamma : context) (theta : atom -> tm) : Prop := forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> C T (theta x)`.

lc_all: `Definition lc_env (theta : atom -> tm) : Prop := forall x, lc_at 0 (theta x)`.

Note: In the T_Abs case, we need to show sat (update Gamma x T1) θ' where θ' = upd_env θ x v:
  intros y T' Hy.
  unfold update in Hy. destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E.
  - Nat.eqb x y = true → x = y (Nat.eqb_eq). Then Hy : Some T1 = Some T' (after substitution?), so T' = T1. Goal C T' (upd_env θ x v y) = C T1 (if x = y then v else θ y) = C T1 v. Need rewrite.
  - Nat.eqb x y = false → Hy : Gamma y = Some T' (since update returns Gamma y). θ' y = θ y (since eqb false). Apply Hsat y T' Hy.
  Use `Nat.eqb_eq`, `Nat.eqb_neq`. Alternatively, use `apply Nat.eqb_eq in E` etc.
  There's a subtlety: `update Gamma x T1 y` unfolds to `if Nat.eqb x y then Some T1 else Gamma y`; destruct the if.

Also, in the T_Abs case, the goal is C (T1->T2) (subst θ (tm_abs T1 t1)), and subst θ (tm_abs T1 t1) = tm_abs T1 (subst θ t1) by definition (simpl). Good.

We need to select a fresh x: `x := fresh (L ++ fv t1)`; then x ∉ L and x ∉ fv t1. Using a concrete fresh function makes the proof constructive and avoids classical choice. Let's define:
```
Fixpoint max_list (L : list atom) : atom :=
  match L with
  | [] => 0
  | y :: ys => Nat.max y (max_list ys)
  end.

Definition fresh (L : list atom) : atom := S (max_list L).

Lemma max_list_bound : forall L y, In y L -> y <= max_list L.
Lemma fresh_notin : forall L, ~ In (fresh L) L.
```
Proof of fresh_notin: intro H; apply max_list_bound in H; unfold fresh in H; lia.

Let's also consider: do we need `has_type` applied with context `empty` and the substitution identity: subst id t = t. Prove `subst_id : forall t, subst (fun x => tm_fvar x) t = t` by induction on t (with simpl; congruence).

Target theorem:
```
Theorem normalization : forall t T,
  <{ empty |-- t \in T }> ->
  strongly_normalizing t.
Proof.
  intros t T Hty.
  pose (theta := fun x => tm_fvar x).
  assert (Hlc : lc_env theta) by (intro x; simpl; constructor).
  assert (Hsat : sat empty theta) by (intros x T' H; unfold empty in H; discriminate).
  pose proof (fundamental empty t T Hty theta Hlc Hsat) as HC.
  rewrite subst_id in HC.
  apply C_sn in HC. exact HC.
Qed.
```
Careful: fundamental's statement with theta as a function; `subst_id` says subst (fun x => tm_fvar x) t = t. ✓.

```
Lemma C_step : forall T t t', C T t -> step t t' -> C T t'.
Proof.
  induction T; intros t t' HC Hstep.
  - simpl in HC. simpl. destruct HC as [Hlc Hsn]. split.
    + apply lc_step with (t := t); assumption.
    + apply SN_step with (t := t); assumption.
  - simpl in HC. destruct HC as [Hlc [Hsn Happ]].
    simpl. split; [apply lc_step with t; assumption | split].
    + apply SN_step with t; assumption.
    + intros u Hu. apply IHT2 with (t := tm_app t u). (* IH for B = T2 *)
      * apply Happ; assumption.
      * apply ST_App1; [assumption | apply C_lc; assumption].
Qed.
```

Let me be careful: Coq's `induction T` on a goal `forall t t', C T t -> ...` with T a variable in the context: it reverts the goal, does the induction, and reintroduces. Potential pitfalls:
- The custom scopes/notations: the file uses custom entries. Our added definitions outside notation scopes should be fine. We should not modify the supplied definitions/theorem statement. We can insert our code before the Theorem normalization and replace the Proof body. The task says "Edit Task.v directly and finish the target theorem... Do not modify Task.v.orig." It doesn't forbid editing the theorem's proof body (obviously). We must keep the theorem statement unchanged.

- The theorem `normalization` must be inside the module.

- Name clashes: `C`, `fv`, `subst`, `fresh`, `max_list`, `sat`, `lc_env`, `upd_env`. Avoid clashing with stdlib names imported. `subst` might conflict with something? There's `subst` tactic but not a definition; naming a definition `subst` might shadow/parse issues when using the `subst` tactic! Indeed, if we define a function named `subst`, then the tactic `subst` might become ambiguous (the tactic `subst` is a keyword-ish tactic, not a term; Coq allows both? The tactic `subst` is fine, but `subst x` might resolve to the tactic. Defining a term named subst could conflict with the tactic name resolution in some cases, but typically tactics and terms live in different namespaces. To be safe, name it `subst_tm` or `substf`. Let's use `subst_tm`. Or `tsubst`. I'll use `subst_tm`.

- `open` is already defined; `open_rec`; fine.

- `update` is for contexts; we need an environment update `upd_env`.

- Notations: inside `stlc_scope`, terms are written with custom notation. In our proofs, we can write `tm_app a b` directly or use notations. Since we're inside the module and scope is open, custom entries only apply inside `<{ }>`, so writing `tm_app` is fine. Careful: the notation `"x y" := (tm_app x y)` in custom stlc_tm only applies in that entry. Plain `tm_app` constructor is accessible. `Bool` notation is only in custom entry; in plain terms, use `Ty_Bool`.

- `tm_abs` constructor: `tm_abs T body`.

- The inductive step's side conditions: ST_App1 requires `locally_closed t2` i.e. `lc_at 0 t2`. ST_AppAbs requires `locally_closed (tm_abs T t)` and `value v`.

```coq
(** ... existing ... *)

(* We insert after strongly_normalizing definition, before Theorem normalization. *)

(** Free variables of a term (as a list). *)
Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  end.

Fixpoint max_list (L : list atom) : atom :=
  match L with
  | nil => 0
  | x :: xs => Nat.max x (max_list xs)
  end.

Definition fresh (L : list atom) : atom := S (max_list L).

Lemma max_list_bound : forall L x, In x L -> x <= max_list L.
Proof.
  induction L as [| y ys IH]; intros x Hin.
  - inversion Hin.
  - simpl in Hin. destruct Hin as [Heq | Hin].
    + subst x. apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + apply Nat.le_trans with (max_list ys); [apply IH; assumption | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma fresh_notin : forall L, ~ In (fresh L) L.
Proof.
  intros L Hin. unfold fresh in Hin.
  pose proof (max_list_bound L (S (max_list L)) Hin) as Hle.
  pose proof (Nat.lt_succ_diag_r (max_list L)) as Hlt.
  apply Nat.lt_irrefl with (max_list L).
  apply Nat.lt_le_trans with (S (max_list L)); assumption.
Qed.
```
Check `Nat.le_max_l` signatures: `Nat.le_max_l : forall n m : nat, n <= Nat.max n m`. ✓. `Nat.le_max_r : forall n m, m <= Nat.max n m`. `simpl` on `max_list (y::ys)` gives `Nat.max y (max_list ys)`. ✓.

Substitution:
```coq
Definition upd_env (theta : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (v : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then v else theta y.

Fixpoint subst_tm (theta : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => theta x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_tm theta t1) (subst_tm theta t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_tm theta t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.

Lemma subst_tm_id : forall t, subst_tm (fun x => tm_fvar x) t = t.
Proof. induction t; simpl; congruence. Qed.
```

```coq

Draft:

```coq
(** ** Auxiliary definitions and lemmas for the normalization proof *)

(** Free variables of a term, as a list. *)
Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  end.

Fixpoint max_list (L : list atom) : atom :=
  match L with
  | nil => 0
  | x :: xs => Nat.max x (max_list xs)
  end.

Definition fresh (L : list atom) : atom := S (max_list L).

Lemma max_list_bound : forall L x, In x L -> x <= max_list L.
Proof.
  induction L as [| y ys IH]; intros x Hin.
  - destruct Hin.
  - simpl in Hin. destruct Hin as [-> | Hin].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + apply Nat.le_trans with (max_list ys); [apply IH; assumption | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma fresh_notin : forall L, ~ In (fresh L) L.
Proof.
  intros L Hin. unfold fresh in Hin.
  pose proof (max_list_bound _ _ Hin). lia.
Qed.
```
Hmm `destruct Hin` on empty list: Hin : In x nil; destruct Hin works (no constructors). ✓.

```coq
(** Free-variable substitution with an environment.  Since all
    environments used in the proof map variables to locally closed
    terms, this naive substitution is safe. *)
Definition upd_env (theta : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (v : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then v else theta y.

Fixpoint subst_tm (theta : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => theta x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_tm theta t1) (subst_tm theta t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_tm theta t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.

Lemma subst_tm_id : forall t, subst_tm (fun x => tm_fvar x) t = t.
Proof. induction t; simpl; congruence. Qed.
```

Alternative approach: prove lc_at_mono using the induction principle of lc_at with an explicit generalized motive. The generated lc_at_ind:
```
lc_at_ind : forall P : nat -> tm -> Prop,
  (forall k i, i < k -> P k (tm_bvar i)) ->
  (forall k x, P k (tm_fvar x)) ->
  (forall k t1 t2, lc_at k t1 -> P k t1 -> lc_at k t2 -> P k t2 -> P k (tm_app t1 t2)) ->
  (forall k T t1, lc_at (S k) t1 -> P (S k) t1 -> P k (tm_abs T t1)) ->
  (forall k, P k tm_true) ->
  (forall k, P k tm_false) ->
  forall k t, lc_at k t -> P k t.
```
Then prove `forall k t, lc_at k t -> forall k', k <= k' -> lc_at k' t` by `induction 1` with a motive that generalizes k'. We can do:
```
Lemma lc_at_mono : forall k t, lc_at k t -> forall k', k <= k' -> lc_at k' t.
Proof.
  intros k t H. induction H as [k i Hi | k x | k a b Ha IHa Hb IHb | k T b Hb IHb | k | k]; intros k' Hle.
  - apply lc_bvar; lia.
  - apply lc_fvar.
  - apply lc_app; [apply IHa; lia | apply IHb; lia].
  - apply lc_abs. apply IHb. lia.  (* IHb : forall k', S k <= k' -> lc_at k' b? *)
```
Hmm, the IH for the abs constructor: the recursive premise is lc_at (S k) t1, and the generated IH would be `IHb : P (S k) t1` for the motive P. If the motive P k t := forall k', k <= k' -> lc_at k' t, then IHb : forall k', S k <= k' -> lc_at k' t1. In the goal (after intros k' Hle), need lc_at (S k') t1. So apply IHb with (S k') and show S k <= S k' from k <= k' via lia. ✓.
This works cleanly! Use the induction principle directly:

Lemma lc_at_mono : forall k t, lc_at k t -> forall k', k <= k' -> lc_at k' t.

Now `SN_step`:
```
Lemma SN_step : forall t t', strongly_normalizing t -> step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t'.
Proof. intros t t' H. induction H as [t Hreds]. intros Hs. apply Hreds. assumption. Qed.
```

```
But `destruct H as [t Hreds]` when H : SN t — the constructor argument is a function; destruct gives Hreds : forall t', step t t' -> SN t'. However, `destruct` on a Prop with one constructor that has a function argument works? `destruct H` will eliminate into Prop (our goal is Prop), fine. But the binder `t` in the pattern: SN_intro's argument is implicit? Let's see the definition:
```
Inductive strongly_normalizing : tm -> Prop :=
  | SN_intro : forall t, (forall t', t --> t' -> strongly_normalizing t') -> strongly_normalizing t.
```
So SN_intro : forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> SN t') -> SN t. Destruct H : SN t gives Hreds : forall t', step t t' -> SN t' (the `t` in the constructor unifies with our t). Pattern `[t Hreds]` names the constructor's t, but unification with the index makes them equal; Coq may error on pattern arity/names? Use `destruct H as [Hreds]`? With `induction H`, Coq typically handles it. Let's use:
```
Lemma SN_step : forall t t', strongly_normalizing t -> step t t' -> strongly_normalizing t'.
Proof.
  intros t t' Hsn Hstep.
  induction Hsn as [t0 Hreds].
  exact (Hreds t' Hstep).
Qed.
```
Here `induction Hsn as [t0 Hreds]` gives Hreds : forall t', step t0 t' -> SN t'. But t0 should be convertible to t; with the index, induction abstracts. Now the C relation:
```
Fixpoint C (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => lc_at 0 t /\ strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow A B => lc_at 0 t /\ strongly_normalizing t /\ (forall u, C A u -> C B (tm_app t u))
  end.
```

Now the big fundamental theorem. Let's restate:

```
Definition lc_env (theta : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall x, lc_at 0 (theta x).

Definition sat (Gamma : context) (theta : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> C T (theta x).

Lemma fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall theta, lc_env theta -> sat Gamma theta -> C T (subst_tm theta t).
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T Hty.
  induction Hty as [Gamma x T Hx
                 | L Gamma T1 T2 t1 Hpre IHpre
                 | Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2 Ht1 IH1 Ht2 IH2
                 | Gamma | Gamma];
  intros theta Hlc Hsat.
```

From Stdlib Require Import Arith.PeanoNat.
From Stdlib Require Import Lists.List.
From Stdlib Require Import Relations.Relation_Definitions.
From Stdlib Require Import Lia.

Module STLCNormalizationNoneHardTask.

... (unchanged) ...

(** * Auxiliary development for the strong normalization proof *)

(** ** Fresh atoms from a finite list *)

Fixpoint fresh_bound (L : list atom) : atom :=
  match L with
  | nil => 0
  | x :: xs => Nat.max x (fresh_bound xs)
  end.

Definition fresh_atom (L : list atom) : atom := S (fresh_bound L).

Lemma fresh_bound_ge : forall L x, In x L -> x <= fresh_bound L.
Proof.
  induction L as [| y ys IH]; intros x Hin.
  - destruct Hin.
  - simpl in Hin. destruct Hin as [-> | Hin].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + apply Nat.le_trans with (fresh_bound ys); [apply IH; exact Hin | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma fresh_atom_notin : forall L, ~ In (fresh_atom L) L.
Proof.
  intros L Hin. unfold fresh_atom in Hin.
  pose proof (fresh_bound_ge L (S (fresh_bound L)) Hin). lia.
Qed.

Lemma notin_app : forall x A B, ~ In x (A ++ B) -> ~ In x A /\ ~ In x B.
Proof.
  intros x A B H. split; intro Hi; apply H; apply in_or_app; [left | right]; exact Hi.
Qed.
```

```
Definition lc_env (theta : atom -> tm) : Prop := forall x, lc_at 0 (theta x).

Definition sat (Gamma : context) (theta : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall x T, Gamma x = Some T -> C T (theta x).

Lemma fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall theta, lc_env theta -> sat Gamma theta -> C T (subst_tm theta t).
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T Hty.
  induction Hty as
    [ Gamma x T Hx
    | L Gamma T1 T2 t1 Hpre IHpre
    | Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2 Ht1 IH1 Ht2 IH2
    | Gamma
    | Gamma ];
    intros theta Hlc Hsat.
```
Hmm, the `as` pattern for the induction: the number of arguments per constructor:
- T_Var : forall Gamma x T, Gamma x = Some T -> has_type ... : 4 args (Gamma x T Hx).
- T_Abs : forall (L : list atom) Gamma T1 T2 t1, (forall x, ...) -> has_type ... : 6 args. Since the IH is generated as a separate hypothesis? In the `as` pattern we can name the premises and IHs. The pattern for T_Abs would be `L Gamma T1 T2 t1 Hpre IHpre` — I'm assuming Coq generates exactly one IH for the nested recursive premise. Let's test.
- T_App : forall Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2, has_type... -> has_type... -> has_type... : 7 args with 2 IHs.
- T_True/T_False: forall Gamma: 1 arg.

(** ** Strong normalization proof *)

(** The proof follows the standard Tait-style reducibility argument.
    The logical relation is closed under forward reduction and under
    "neutral expansion"; the crucial call-by-value subtlety is that a
    beta-redex whose argument is not yet a value is neutral, so we can
    use the neutral-expansion property to prove it reducible. *)

(** *** Fresh atoms *)

Fixpoint fresh_bound (L : list atom) : atom :=
  match L with
  | nil => 0
  | x :: xs => Nat.max x (fresh_bound xs)
  end.

Definition fresh_atom (L : list atom) : atom := S (fresh_bound L).

Lemma fresh_bound_ge : forall L x, In x L -> x <= fresh_bound L.
Proof.
  induction L as [| y ys IH]; intros x Hin.
  - destruct Hin.
  - simpl in Hin. destruct Hin as [-> | Hin].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + apply Nat.le_trans with (fresh_bound ys); [apply IH; exact Hin | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma fresh_atom_notin : forall L, ~ In (fresh_atom L) L.
Proof.
  intros L Hin. unfold fresh_atom in Hin.
  pose proof (fresh_bound_ge L (S (fresh_bound L)) Hin). lia.
Qed.

Lemma notin_app : forall x A B, ~ In x (A ++ B) -> ~ In x A /\ ~ In x B.
Proof.
  intros x A B H. split; intro Hi; apply H; apply in_or_app;
    [left | right]; exact Hi.
Qed.

(** *** Free variables and substitution *)

Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  end.

Definition upd_env (theta : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (v : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then v else theta y.

Fixpoint subst_tm (theta : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => theta x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_tm theta t1) (subst_tm theta t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_tm theta t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.

Lemma subst_tm_id : forall t, subst_tm (fun x => tm_fvar x) t = t.
Proof. induction t; simpl; congruence. Qed.

Definition lc_env (theta : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall x, lc_at 0 (theta x).
```

(** ** Strong normalization proof *)

(** *** Fresh atoms *)

Fixpoint fresh_bound (L : list atom) : atom :=
  match L with
  | nil => 0
  | x :: xs => Nat.max x (fresh_bound xs)
  end.

Definition fresh_atom (L : list atom) : atom := S (fresh_bound L).

Proof.
  induction L as [| y ys IH]; intros x Hin.
  - destruct Hin.
  - simpl in Hin. destruct Hin as [-> | Hin].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + apply Nat.le_trans with (fresh_bound ys); [apply IH; exact Hin | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma fresh_atom_notin : forall L, ~ In (fresh_atom L) L.
Proof.
  intros L Hin. unfold fresh_atom in Hin.
  pose proof (fresh_bound_ge L (S (fresh_bound L)) Hin). lia.
Qed.

Proof.
  intros x A B H. split; intro Hi; apply H; apply in_or_app;
    [left | right]; exact Hi.
Qed.

(** *** Free variables and substitution *)

Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  end.

  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then v else theta y.

  upd_env theta x v x = v.

  y <> x -> upd_env theta x v y = theta y.
Proof.
  intros theta x y v H. unfold upd_env.
  apply Nat.eqb_neq in H. rewrite H. reflexivity.
Qed.

Substitution-opening identity:

```coq
Lemma subst_open_rec : forall k t theta v x,
  ~ In x (fv t) ->
  (forall y, In y (fv t) -> lc_at 0 (theta y)) ->
  subst_tm (upd_env theta x v) (open_rec k (tm_fvar x) t)
    = open_rec k v (subst_tm theta t).
Proof.
  induction t as [n | y | t1 IH1 t2 IH2 | T t1 IH1 | | ];
    intros k theta v x Hfresh Hlc.
  - simpl. destruct (Nat.eqb k n) eqn:E.
    + simpl. unfold upd_env. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. reflexivity.
    + reflexivity.
  - simpl. unfold upd_env. destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E.
    + apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst y. exfalso. apply Hfresh. simpl. left. reflexivity.
    + reflexivity.
  - simpl. f_equal; [apply IH1 | apply IH2]; assumption.
  - simpl. f_equal. apply IH1 with (k := S k) (x := x); assumption.
  - simpl. reflexivity.
  - simpl. reflexivity.
Qed.
```
Let me check each case.
- bvar n: `open_rec k (fvar x) (bvar n)` = if eqb k n then fvar x else bvar n. LHS subst: if eqb k n then subst (upd_env θ x v) (fvar x) = upd_env θ x v x = v, else bvar n. simpl: goal after simpl? Let's see: `simpl` unfolds open_rec and subst_tm. In the eqb k n = true branch: LHS = subst_tm (upd_env θ x v) (tm_fvar x) which simpl reduces to upd_env θ x v x. RHS = open_rec k v (subst_tm θ (bvar n)) = open_rec k v (bvar n) = if eqb k n then v else bvar n = v. So goal upd_env θ x v x = v. unfold upd_env; rewrite Nat.eqb_refl; reflexivity. ✓.
  In the false branch: LHS = bvar n; RHS = open_rec k v (bvar n) = bvar n. reflexivity. ✓.
  But careful: after `destruct (Nat.eqb k n) eqn:E`, the goal has both the LHS and RHS occurrences of the same eqb; destruct rewrites both. Then in the true branch we need to reduce `subst_tm (upd_env ...) (tm_fvar x)`: simpl should. And `upd_env θ x v x`: the definition uses Nat.eqb x x; unfold then rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. But if simpl reduces Nat.eqb x x to true directly (Nat.eqb is a Fixpoint on x and x; `simpl` may reduce it), then reflexivity might work. Let's keep the rewrite.
  Hmm, careful: after `unfold upd_env`, the goal is `(if Nat.eqb x x then v else θ x) = v`. `rewrite Nat.eqb_refl` changes `Nat.eqb x x` to true (Nat.eqb_refl : forall x, (x =? x) = true). Then simpl/reflexivity. But if the goal already simplified to v = v, then `unfold upd_env` may fail ("nothing to unfold")? Actually unfold works even if it's already... hmm, if simpl already reduced it, `unfold upd_env` would fail with "Cannot unfold". To be safe, write the branch proof as:
  ```

```
Lemma fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall theta, lc_env theta -> sat Gamma theta -> C T (subst_tm theta t).
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T Hty.
  induction Hty as
    [ Gamma x T Hx
    | L Gamma T1 T2 t1 Hpre IHpre
    | Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2 Ht1 IH1 Ht2 IH2
    | Gamma
    | Gamma ];
    intros theta Hlc Hsat.
  - (* T_Var *)
    simpl. exact (Hsat x T Hx).
  - (* T_Abs *)
    simpl. split.
    + (* local closure *)
      apply lc_abs.
      assert (Hlc1 : lc_at 1 t1).
      { apply lc_at_open_inv with (k := 0) (u := tm_fvar (fresh_atom L)).
        exact (has_type_lc _ _ _ (Hpre (fresh_atom L) (fresh_atom_notin L))). }
      apply lc_at_subst with (t := t1) (k := 1). exact Hlc1. exact Hlc.
    + split.
      * apply SN_abs.
      * intros u Hu.
        apply C_beta with (A := T1) (B := T2) (body := subst_tm theta t1) (u := u).
        -- (* lc_at 1 body *)
           apply lc_at_subst with (t := t1) (k := 1).
           ++ apply lc_at_open_inv with (k := 0) (u := tm_fvar (fresh_atom L)).
              exact (has_type_lc _ _ _ (Hpre (fresh_atom L) (fresh_atom_notin L))).
           ++ exact Hlc.
        -- (* Hbody *)
           intros v Hv.
           set (x := fresh_atom (L ++ fv t1)).
           assert (HxL : ~ In x L).
           { unfold x. intro Hi. apply (fresh_atom_notin (L ++ fv t1)). apply in_or_app. left. exact Hi. }
           assert (Hxfv : ~ In x (fv t1)).
           { unfold x. intro Hi. apply (fresh_atom_notin (L ++ fv t1)). apply in_or_app. right. exact Hi. }
           set (theta' := upd_env theta x v).
           assert (Hlc' : lc_env theta').
           { intro y. unfold theta', upd_env. destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E.
             - apply C_lc with (T := T1). exact Hv.
             - apply Hlc. }
           assert (Hsat' : sat (update Gamma x T1) theta').
           { intros y T' Hy. unfold update in Hy. unfold theta', upd_env.
             destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E.
             - apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst y.
               (* Hy : Some T1 = Some T'? or (if eqb x x then Some T1 else Gamma x) = Some T'; simpl in Hy *)
               apply Nat.eqb_eq in E? -- E is already eqb x y = true, gives x = y.
               ...
             - apply Nat.eqb_neq in E.
               (* Hy : Gamma y = Some T' *)
               apply Hsat. exact Hy. }
           pose proof (IHpre x HxL theta' Hlc' Hsat') as Hres.
           (* Hres : C T2 (subst_tm theta' (open t1 (fvar x))) *)
           rewrite (subst_open_rec 0 t1 theta v x Hxfv _) in Hres.
           ...
```
Hmm wait, IHpre's argument order: the induction principle gives `(forall x, ~ In x L -> P (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2)` where P is the motive `fun Gamma t T => forall theta, lc_env theta -> sat Gamma theta -> C T (subst_tm theta t)`. So IHpre x HxL : forall theta, lc_env theta -> sat (update Gamma x T1) theta -> C T2 (subst_tm theta (open t1 (fvar x))). Then rewrite with subst_open_rec: the LHS of the lemma with k:=0, t:=t1, theta:=theta, v:=v, x:=x is `subst_tm (upd_env theta x v) (open_rec 0 (tm_fvar x) t1)`. Rewrite may not see it as `open_rec 0 ...` unless we `unfold open` or `cbn`. Since `open` is a Definition, `simpl`/`cbn` would unfold it. To make the rewrite match, do `change`:
```
change (C T2 (subst_tm theta' (open_rec 0 (tm_fvar x) t1))) in Hres.
rewrite (subst_open_rec 0 t1 theta v x Hxfv Hlc_fv) in Hres.
```
The `change` should work by delta-reduction of `open`. Then after rewriting, Hres : C T2 (open_rec 0 v (subst_tm theta t1)). Then:
```
exact Hres.
```
And the goal at that point: C T2 (open (subst_tm theta t1) v) which is convertible to open_rec 0 v (subst_tm theta t1). ✓.

The Hlc_fv premise: forall y, In y (fv t1) -> lc_at 0 (theta y). From Hlc: intro y Hy; apply Hlc. So `exact Hlc`? Hlc : lc_env theta = forall x, lc_at 0 (theta x). The required type is forall y, In y (fv t1) -> lc_at 0 (theta y). `intros y Hy; apply Hlc.` So:
```
rewrite (subst_open_rec 0 t1 theta v x Hxfv (fun y Hy => Hlc y)) in Hres.
```
✓.

IH1 : P Gamma t1 (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) = forall theta, lc_env theta -> sat Gamma theta -> C (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) (subst_tm theta t1). So `IH1 theta Hlc Hsat`. ✓.
C_app's argument order: forall A B t, C (A->B) t -> forall u, C A u -> C B (app t u). So `C_app T1 T2 (subst_tm theta t1) (IH1 ...) (subst_tm theta t2) (IH2 ...)`. ✓.

The statement is `A \/ B \/ C` which parses as `A \/ (B \/ C)`. Let me write out:

```
(** * Strong normalization proof *)

(** We use a Tait-style reducibility relation [C T t].  It is defined
    on terms that may contain free variables; free variables are
    interpreted by environments in the fundamental lemma.  The two key
    closure properties are closure under one-step reduction and the
    "neutral expansion" property: an irreducible-for-now application
    whose one-step reducts are all reducible is reducible. *)

(** ** Fresh atoms *)

Fixpoint fresh_bound (L : list atom) : atom :=
  match L with
  | nil => 0
  | x :: xs => Nat.max x (fresh_bound xs)
  end.

Definition fresh_atom (L : list atom) : atom := S (fresh_bound L).

Lemma fresh_bound_ge : forall L x, In x L -> x <= fresh_bound L.
Proof.
  induction L as [| y ys IH]; intros x Hin.
  - destruct Hin.
  - simpl in Hin. destruct Hin as [-> | Hin].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + apply Nat.le_trans with (fresh_bound ys); [apply IH; exact Hin | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma fresh_atom_notin : forall L, ~ In (fresh_atom L) L.
Proof.
  intros L Hin. unfold fresh_atom in Hin.
  pose proof (fresh_bound_ge L (S (fresh_bound L)) Hin). lia.
Qed.

(** ** Free variables, environments and substitution *)

Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  end.

Definition upd_env (theta : atom -> tm) (x : atom) (v : tm) : atom -> tm :=
  fun y => if Nat.eqb x y then v else theta y.

Definition lc_env (theta : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall x, lc_at 0 (theta x).

Fixpoint subst_tm (theta : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => theta x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_tm theta t1) (subst_tm theta t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_tm theta t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.

Lemma subst_tm_id : forall t, subst_tm (fun x => tm_fvar x) t = t.
Proof. induction t; simpl; congruence. Qed.

Lemma upd_env_eq : forall theta x v, upd_env theta x v x = v.
Proof. intros. unfold upd_env. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. reflexivity. Qed.

Lemma upd_env_neq : forall theta x y v, x <> y -> upd_env theta x v y = theta y.
Proof.
  intros theta x y v H. unfold upd_env.
  apply Nat.eqb_neq in H. rewrite H. reflexivity.
Qed.
```

Hmm, `apply Nat.eqb_neq in H` where H : x <> y and goal is... wait we're in a proof of an equality goal, H is a hypothesis. `apply ... in H` requires the lemma's conclusion to match H? The tactic `apply lemma in H` uses the lemma to transform H; if the lemma is an iff (A <-> B) and H : B, it produces H : A? Coq's `apply ... in` works with implications; for iff it might need direction. Let me instead prove upd_env_neq with:
```
Proof.
  intros theta x y v H. unfold upd_env.
  destruct (Nat.eqb x y) eqn:E.
  - apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst y. exfalso. exact (H eq_refl).
  - reflexivity.
Qed.
```
This avoids the in-direction issue. ✓.

And upd_env_eq via `rewrite Nat.eqb_refl`: Nat.eqb_refl : forall x, (x =? x) = true. The goal `(if (x =? x) then v else theta x) = v`. `rewrite Nat.eqb_refl.` rewrites to `(if true then v else theta x) = v`, then `reflexivity` (simpl reduces if true).
